I have to make noise or move or talk to somebody.\n\n[Last Question]\nCan you tell me if you've noticed these same difficulties—like trouble focusing, feeling restless, or acting without thinking—in at least two other distinct situations or environments outside of work and home, such as when you're out running errands, interacting with strangers, or during structured activities like classes or group events?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / production_worker / 9th_12th_no_diploma\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like... I mean, I know I just kind of said this a minute ago, but I really, truly don't have any other places to tell you about. You're asking for two other distinct situations, but my life just doesn't have two other situations in it. 

Like, you mentioned classes? I haven't been in a classroom since I dropped out. I couldn't handle sitting at a desk all day back then, and there is absolutely no way I'm signing up for any kind of structured class now. Plus, working the shifts I do at the plant, when would I even go to a class? I'm either on the production line, or I'm at home trying to recover from being on the production line. 

And group events... I don't really do those either. Honestly, by the time the weekend rolls around, my whole body is just completely physically exhausted from standing on that concrete floor all week, but my brain is still doing a million miles an hour. So I just end up pacing around my tiny apartment or messing with my phone. But you said outside of home and work, so that doesn't count for what you're asking. 

For errands, I literally only go to the grocery store. And like I told you before, I'm pushing a cart the whole time, so I'm constantly walking and grabbing boxes and throwing them in the cart, so I don't get that awful trapped feeling there. I don't go to the bank, I just use the app on my phone. I don't go to the post office. I don't go to the movies because sitting in the dark for two hours sounds like absolute torture, so I haven't done that in years. I just automatically avoid anywhere that I know is gonna make me feel like I have to stay put or follow rules about being quiet. 

So, yeah, I really don't interact with strangers or go to structured things. It's literally just work, the bus, and my apartment. I don't know what else to tell you because I just don't put myself in any other environments to even know if I'd have trouble focusing or feeling restless there. There's just... there's really nowhere else.

My supervisor is the one who called it a major safety problem, which really freaked me out, honestly, because I don't want to get hurt or get anyone else hurt just because I can't stay in my chair. But it's just like I told you, I take off before I even think about it. \n\nAnd since you mentioned watching TV, it's funny you say that, because it happens at home all the time, too. I'll try to sit down and watch a movie, like a movie I actually really want to watch, and I just cannot stay on the couch. I'll get up to get water, or look out the window, or I'll just end up standing behind the couch for half the movie watching it from back there. My roommate is always telling me to just sit down and relax, and I want to, I really do, but it's like my legs just decide it's time to go and my brain hasn't caught up yet. \n\nBut yeah, the work thing is the most stressful part right now because of the heavy machinery and all the forklifts driving around. I just... I don't know how to make myself stay put like a normal person. It’s like I’m on autopilot, but the autopilot just wants to walk around.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you also find yourself struggling to focus on tasks, getting distracted easily, or acting without thinking things through in other parts of your life, like at home, with friends, or when you're trying to follow instructions?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, the focusing thing is definitely a huge issue for me, especially at work. Like, okay, so besides being on the line, sometimes they have me do these inventory logs, right? Which sounds easy enough, just counting parts and writing down numbers. And I really, really try to do it right. I sit down with the clipboard and the pen, and I tell myself, \"Okay, we're just going to count these boxes and write it down, no big deal.\" And for the first, like, two or three minutes, I'm doing it. I'm counting. But then... it's like my brain just slips a gear or something. The numbers start to literally blur together on the page. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with the training materials. They're trying to train me on the CNC machines, which is actually a really good opportunity because it pays a little more, and you get to learn a real skill. So they give you these training pages to read through before you actually get on the machine. And I want to learn it, I really do! I'll be staring at the page, reading the words, but my eyes are just skimming over them. I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. Nothing stuck. Not a single word. So then I have to go all the way back to the top and start over, and it's just so frustrating because I feel like I'm trying so hard, but it's like my brain just refuses to hold onto the information. I'll read the same paragraph like five times and it still doesn't make sense because I just can't keep my attention on it. And then I start worrying that I'm taking too long, and my supervisor is going to think I'm slacking off, but I'm not! I'm really trying, but it just won't go in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had similar issues with staying focused or staying seated in other settings, like when you were in school or during social gatherings with friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, absolutely. School was... well, school was a disaster for me, honestly. That's a big part of why I didn't finish high school. But even way back, like, I'm talking elementary school, I was always *that* kid. You know, the one the teacher is constantly having to redirect? I remember being in, like, second or third grade, and everyone else would be sitting cross-legged on the carpet for story time, perfectly still, and I just couldn't do it. I'd be up and wandering over to the class pet's cage, or looking out the window, or just pacing at the back of the room. My teachers were always so frustrated with me. \n\nAnd it wasn't just the wandering around, either. I was always talking over the teacher, too. Like, they'd ask a question to the class, and instead of raising my hand and waiting to be called on like a normal kid, the answer would just fly out of my mouth. I couldn't stop it. I'd just blurt things out constantly. Or I'd interrupt other kids when they were talking because a thought popped into my head and I felt like I had to say it right that exact second or I'd explode. \n\nMy mom used to get so many notes sent home. Like, constantly. \"She won't stay in her seat,\" \"She's disrupting the class,\" \"She's blurting things out and not waiting her turn.\" It was always the same thing. And my mom would get mad, and I'd get grounded, and I'd promise I'd do better the next day, and I really meant it! I'd go to school thinking, \"Okay, today I'm going to sit still and be quiet,\" but then five minutes into math class, I'm already out of my chair looking at a poster on the wall or talking to the kid next to me. \n\nAnd social stuff with friends... yeah, it happens there, too. If we go out to eat or something, I'm the one who goes to the bathroom three times just so I can walk around, or I'll stand by the table instead of sitting in the booth. I just... I've always been like this. It's just that now, with the forklifts and everything at work, it's actually dangerous, whereas in elementary school, it just meant I was annoying the teacher, you know? But yeah, it's been happening forever.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these difficulties with staying seated, focusing, or acting impulsively?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, the actual wandering around and blurting things out has basically been happening my whole life. Since I was a little kid. But if you mean when did it start actually ruining things for me, like really causing serious problems where I realized I was in way over my head... that specific level of bad started about a year and two months ago. \n\nI know it has been exactly a year and two months because that was right around the time I decided I really needed to get my act together. Like, since I didn't finish high school, which I told you about, I was just doing the basic line work. And I just decided I need to do better, I need to make real money and have a real career. So I tried to really tighten up my habits and move toward getting my forklift and CNC certifications. I wanted to prove to my supervisor and to myself that I could handle the heavy machinery and the extra responsibility. \n\nBut that is exactly when everything just kind of fell apart. Because it is one thing to be the annoying kid in third grade who won't stay on the reading carpet, but it is completely different when you are trying to learn how to operate a machine that can literally crush a pallet of steel, and you can't even force your brain to focus on the safety checklist for two minutes without walking away. So for the last fourteen months, it has just been this constant, exhausting struggle. I keep trying so hard to force myself to be disciplined so I can get this certification, but every single day it feels like I am fighting my own body just to stay at my station and read the manuals. So yeah, about a year and two months of it being a really, really serious issue.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other situations—like when you're out with friends, in public places, or during appointments—where you've noticed the same struggles with staying still, focusing, or acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Oh, for sure. I mean, even if I do manage to actually stay in my seat, which like I said is a huge struggle in itself, I am never just... still. Like, if I have to sit for the certification class—the one I was telling you about for the machines—or even just for our mandatory safety meetings we have to do at the plant, it is impossible for me to just sit there quietly. \n\nMy foot is constantly bouncing. Just up and down, up and down, like a jackhammer. And if I try to force my leg to stop and press my foot flat on the floor, then my hands start going. I will be clicking a pen over and over, not even realizing how loud it is in a quiet room, or I am picking at my sleeves. I wear these long-sleeve thermal shirts under my uniform because the warehouse gets really drafty near the loading docks, and I will just sit there picking at the fabric, pulling at little threads until I completely ruin the cuffs. \n\nAnd I am constantly shifting around in those cheap plastic chairs they have in the breakroom. I am crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning forward, leaning back, sliding down in the seat. It actually gets to the point where I am moving around so much that people ask me to stop. The guy who usually sits next to me during the safety briefs will literally put his hand down to steady things and tell me I am shaking the entire table and ask if I can please just hold still. And it is so embarrassing because I genuinely did not even know I was doing it! \n\nBut I just have all this built-up energy, and if I am not physically walking around, it has to leak out somehow. So then I will try to sit on my hands to stop picking at my shirt or clicking the pen, but then my foot just immediately starts bouncing all over again, and it is just this never-ending cycle of trying not to bother anyone while feeling like my motor is just running and running. It happens at restaurants with friends, too, like I'm playing with the straw wrappers or shaking the booth, but it's the worst at work during those meetings because everyone is so quiet and just staring at the supervisor, and I'm over there practically vibrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding or really struggling to stick with tasks that require a lot of focus or mental effort, like reading something boring, filling out paperwork, or trying to follow complicated instructions?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. That is honestly one of the biggest roadblocks for me right now, and it is so incredibly frustrating. Because, like I was saying before about wanting to get those certifications for the CNC machines and the forklifts? You can't just hop on the equipment and start pulling levers. There is this whole massive binder of training pages you have to get through first, plus all the regular paperwork I already have to do for my current line work, like the inventory logs. \n\nAnd I really, really try. I promise you, I do. I sit down, I tell myself, \"Okay, you're going to read this, you're going to focus, you're going to get this done so you can move up and make better money.\" I start out trying so hard. I'll have my pen ready, I'll be looking right at the page... but then, I don't know, maybe just a few minutes go by, and it's like my brain just completely unplugs itself. The numbers on the inventory sheets or the words in the CNC training manual, they literally just start to blur together. \n\nIt's not my eyes or anything, but it's like I'm suddenly looking at a completely foreign language. I'll be reading, or at least my eyes are moving across the page, and I'll get to the bottom, and I will suddenly realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. Nothing. I didn't take a single word of it in. \n\nSo then I have to go back to the top and start all over again. And I'll do that three, four, five times for the exact same paragraph! It takes me an hour to do something that should take ten minutes because I just cannot hold onto the information. And with the inventory logs at the plant, it's the exact same thing. I'll be counting boxes of parts, and I'll have the clipboard, and I'll look down to write the number, and poof, the numbers just scramble in my head, and I have to recount the whole entire pallet. My supervisor gets so annoyed because I'm always the last one to turn my paperwork in at the end of the shift, but I just... I can't force my brain to stick with it, no matter how much I want to. I'll even try reading out loud to myself to see if that helps, but then I just start thinking about how my voice sounds in the empty warehouse, or if anyone can hear me, and then I've completely lost my place again. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times, outside of work or school, where you've had trouble staying still, focusing, or felt mentally restless, like in a doctor's waiting room, on public transport, or during a religious service?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. I mean, waiting rooms are an absolute nightmare for me, and public transit? Forget about it. If I have to take the bus to the warehouse because my car is acting up or whatever, I am just miserable the entire time. I'll be sitting there in those uncomfortable plastic seats, and the bus is stopping at every single block, and I just feel this crazy urge to stand up and walk down the aisle, even though the bus is moving and you're supposed to stay seated. \n\nBut honestly, even though you asked about outside of work, my mind just keeps going right back to the plant because that is where this feeling is just... it's the most intense, and it's what I'm stressing about the most. Because it's not just a mental thing where I'm bored. When I try to stand still by the machine on the line, or when I'm trying to sit through that certification training I was telling you about... it literally feels like there’s this actual pressure building in my body. \n\nIt's hard to explain, but it's like a soda bottle that someone just shook up as hard as they could, and the cap is still screwed on tight. The carbonation is just expanding, right? That is exactly what my chest and my arms and my legs feel like. It just builds and builds and builds, and it gets so incredibly unbearable that I absolutely have to move, or pace, or stretch, or just go do something right then. I can't just wait five minutes. I can't just tough it out until the end of the shift. \n\nIf I'm standing by the machine waiting for a cycle to finish, which only takes a couple of minutes, that pressure gets so bad I'll start doing these huge, exaggerated stretches, or I'll just start pacing up and down the concrete aisle. Or I'll go find a broom to sweep a corner by the loading dock that's already completely clean, just because I have to do *something* physical right at that exact second to let the pressure out. It’s like my brain is screaming at me that standing still is physically impossible. So yeah, it happens on the bus and at the doctor's office and stuff, but standing at my station or sitting in that training room when that pressure hits... it's just torture.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you've had trouble staying still or focusing in situations like religious services, waiting rooms, or public transportation?\n\nPatient: I mean... It's like the quiet itself is just... it's like a cage, you know? I just can't do it. Oh, god, yeah. Definitely. I mean, honestly, it was probably even worse back then because I didn't have any control over where I was or what I was doing, you know? Like, in elementary school, I vividly remember third and fourth grade just being an absolute nightmare for me and my teachers.

And the talking... oh man, the talking. I talked over the teacher constantly. Like, they would be right in the middle of a sentence explaining a math problem or reading a story, and if I knew the answer, or honestly even if I just had some completely random thought pop into my head about what we were having for lunch, I would just blurt it out. I didn't even raise my hand. I didn't even realize I was doing it half the time until the teacher would stop talking and just glare at me, and the whole class would turn around and look. 

My mom used to get so mad because I got notes sent home constantly. Like, it felt like almost every single week there was a new note stapled to my homework folder about how I was being "disruptive" or blurting things out during reading time or refusing to stay seated during lessons. It was just this endless cycle of getting in trouble, getting a note sent home, getting yelled at by my mom, and then going back the next day and doing the exact same thing because I just couldn't stop myself. So, yeah, this isn't a new thing at all. It’s basically been happening my entire life, way before I was twelve.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, yeah, absolutely. It's not always just my body needing to move, sometimes it's like my brain just completely unplugs itself even if I'm standing perfectly still. 

Like, at the plant, sometimes my supervisor will have me do these inventory logs. It usually happens when a machine goes down and we're just waiting on maintenance, so they hand me this beat-up clipboard and tell me to go count boxes of fasteners or brackets or whatever in the storage cage. And I'm just standing there, not pacing, not moving around, just staring at the clipboard. I start out really trying, you know? I tell myself, "Okay, just focus, count the boxes, write the number down, get it done." 

But then... I don't know, it's like after just a few minutes, the numbers just completely lose all meaning. The lines on the paper start to blur together. I'll count a stack of boxes, look down at the sheet, and realize I completely forgot the number I literally just counted three seconds ago. 

And it's the exact same thing with that certification manual I was telling you about earlier. The CNC training pages. I really, really want to pass this test because getting certified on the CNC machines means a pretty decent pay bump, and my landlord just raised my rent again, so I really need the money. So I will sit there, forcing myself to be still, staring right at the training pages. I'm looking at the words, my eyes are moving across the sentences, I'm reading about feed rates and spindle speeds and tolerances... but my brain isn't actually absorbing a single thing. 

After a couple of minutes, the words just turn into gray mush on the page. They just blur together. I'll get all the way to the bottom of a really dense page about, I don't know, tool offsets, and I'll suddenly realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. None. I didn't take any of it in. It's like my eyes were reading but my brain was on another planet. So then I have to go all the way back to the top of the page and start over. And then I'll do it again! I'll read the exact same paragraph four or five times in a row, just trying to force the information into my head, but it just won't stick. It is so incredibly frustrating because I am trying so hard, I'm putting in the effort, I'm not even moving, but it's like there's a wall in my head that just blocks the information from actually getting in.

Can you tell me what's been going on that brought you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah, um... so, I work at this production plant, right? And I guess what brought me in is that I'm in a bit of trouble at work. See, I'm on the line, and you have this specific station where you're supposed to stay put for your whole shift, except for your scheduled breaks, obviously. But I just... I keep leaving my station. Like, constantly. I'll just pop up and walk away. \n\nAnd it's not even just out on the floor, it happens during our shift meetings too. We have these morning huddles in the breakroom before we clock in, and everyone else is just sitting there in those cheap plastic chairs listening to the shift lead, and I just can't. I end up popping up out of my seat and standing in the back or pacing around when I'm really, really supposed to just stay put and listen. \n\nMy supervisor—he's usually pretty chill, but he's getting really frustrated with me—he pulled me aside the other day and said it's actually becoming a major safety problem now. Because there's forklifts and heavy machinery moving around, and I just take off before I even think about it. It’s like my brain doesn't even register that I'm standing up until I'm already walking away from my spot. I don't mean to do it, I just... I just go. And if I lose this job, I don't know what I'll do, you know? Since I didn't finish high school, it's hard to find good-paying factory jobs like this one, so I really need to figure out why I can't just keep myself in my chair like everyone else does.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had trouble staying seated or felt the need to get up and move around in situations where others are staying still, like during meetings, classes, or even at home watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah, exactly, like I was just saying! That's exactly it. That's the whole reason I'm here, because of the work thing—leaving my station on the line and popping up during those morning meetings when I am definitely supposed to stay put. My supervisor is the one who called it a major safety problem, which really freaked me out, honestly, because I don't want to get hurt or get anyone else hurt just because I can't stay in my chair. But it's just like I told you, I take off before I even think about it. \n\nAnd since you mentioned watching TV, it's funny you say that, because it happens at home all the time, too. I'll try to sit down and watch a movie, like a movie I actually really want to watch, and I just cannot stay on the couch. I'll get up to get water, or look out the window, or I'll just end up standing behind the couch for half the movie watching it from back there. My roommate is always telling me to just sit down and relax, and I want to, I really do, but it's like my legs just decide it's time to go and my brain hasn't caught up yet. \n\nBut yeah, the work thing is the most stressful part right now because of the heavy machinery and all the forklifts driving around. I just... I don't know how to make myself stay put like a normal person. It’s like I’m on autopilot, but the autopilot just wants to walk around.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you also find yourself struggling to focus on tasks, getting distracted easily, or acting without thinking things through in other parts of your life, like at home, with friends, or when you're trying to follow instructions?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, the focusing thing is definitely a huge issue for me, especially at work. Like, okay, so besides being on the line, sometimes they have me do these inventory logs, right? Which sounds easy enough, just counting parts and writing down numbers. And I really, really try to do it right. I sit down with the clipboard and the pen, and I tell myself, \"Okay, we're just going to count these boxes and write it down, no big deal.\" And for the first, like, two or three minutes, I'm doing it. I'm counting. But then... it's like my brain just slips a gear or something. The numbers start to literally blur together on the page. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with the training materials. They're trying to train me on the CNC machines, which is actually a really good opportunity because it pays a little more, and you get to learn a real skill. So they give you these training pages to read through before you actually get on the machine. And I want to learn it, I really do! I'll be staring at the page, reading the words, but my eyes are just skimming over them. I'll get to the bottom of the page and realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. Nothing stuck. Not a single word. So then I have to go all the way back to the top and start over, and it's just so frustrating because I feel like I'm trying so hard, but it's like my brain just refuses to hold onto the information. I'll read the same paragraph like five times and it still doesn't make sense because I just can't keep my attention on it. And then I start worrying that I'm taking too long, and my supervisor is going to think I'm slacking off, but I'm not! I'm really trying, but it just won't go in.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had similar issues with staying focused or staying seated in other settings, like when you were in school or during social gatherings with friends?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, absolutely. School was... well, school was a disaster for me, honestly. That's a big part of why I didn't finish high school. But even way back, like, I'm talking elementary school, I was always *that* kid. You know, the one the teacher is constantly having to redirect? I remember being in, like, second or third grade, and everyone else would be sitting cross-legged on the carpet for story time, perfectly still, and I just couldn't do it. I'd be up and wandering over to the class pet's cage, or looking out the window, or just pacing at the back of the room. And my mom would get mad, and I'd get grounded, and I'd promise I'd do better the next day, and I really meant it! I'd go to school thinking, \"Okay, today I'm going to sit still and be quiet,\" but then five minutes into math class, I'm already out of my chair looking at a poster on the wall or talking to the kid next to me. \n\nAnd social stuff with friends... yeah, it happens there, too. If we go out to eat or something, I'm the one who goes to the bathroom three times just so I can walk around, or I'll stand by the table instead of sitting in the booth. I just... I've always been like this. It's just that now, with the forklifts and everything at work, it's actually dangerous, whereas in elementary school, it just meant I was annoying the teacher, you know? But yeah, it's been happening forever.\n\nDoctor: When did you first start noticing these difficulties with staying seated, focusing, or acting impulsively?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was just saying, the actual wandering around and blurting things out has basically been happening my whole life. Since I was a little kid. But if you mean when did it start actually ruining things for me, like really causing serious problems where I realized I was in way over my head... that specific level of bad started about a year and two months ago. \n\nI know it has been exactly a year and two months because that was right around the time I decided I really needed to get my act together. Like, since I didn't finish high school, which I told you about, I was just doing the basic line work. And I just decided I need to do better, I need to make real money and have a real career. So I tried to really tighten up my habits and move toward getting my forklift and CNC certifications. I wanted to prove to my supervisor and to myself that I could handle the heavy machinery and the extra responsibility. \n\nBut that is exactly when everything just kind of fell apart. Because it is one thing to be the annoying kid in third grade who won't stay on the reading carpet, but it is completely different when you are trying to learn how to operate a machine that can literally crush a pallet of steel, and you can't even force your brain to focus on the safety checklist for two minutes without walking away. So for the last fourteen months, it has just been this constant, exhausting struggle. I keep trying so hard to force myself to be disciplined so I can get this certification, but every single day it feels like I am fighting my own body just to stay at my station and read the manuals. I wanted to prove to my supervisor and to myself that I could handle the heavy machinery and the extra responsibility. \n\nBut that is exactly when everything just kind of fell apart. Because it is one thing to be the annoying kid in third grade who won't stay on the reading carpet, but it is completely different when you are trying to learn how to operate a machine that can literally crush a pallet of steel, and you can't even force your brain to focus on the safety checklist for two minutes without walking away. So for the last fourteen months, it has just been this constant, exhausting struggle. I keep trying so hard to force myself to be disciplined so I can get this certification, but every single day it feels like I am fighting my own body just to stay at my station and read the manuals. So yeah, about a year and two months of it being a really, really serious issue.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any other situations—like when you're out with friends, in public places, or during appointments—where you've noticed the same struggles with staying still, focusing, or acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Oh, for sure. I mean, even if I do manage to actually stay in my seat, which like I said is a huge struggle in itself, I am never just... still. Like, if I have to sit for the certification class—the one I was telling you about for the machines—or even just for our mandatory safety meetings we have to do at the plant, it is impossible for me to just sit there quietly. \n\nMy foot is constantly bouncing. Just up and down, up and down, like a jackhammer. And if I try to force my leg to stop and press my foot flat on the floor, then my hands start going. I will be clicking a pen over and over, not even realizing how loud it is in a quiet room, or I am picking at my sleeves. I wear these long-sleeve thermal shirts under my uniform because the warehouse gets really drafty near the loading docks, and I will just sit there picking at the fabric, pulling at little threads until I completely ruin the cuffs. \n\nAnd I am constantly shifting around in those cheap plastic chairs they have in the breakroom. I am crossing my legs, uncrossing them, leaning forward, leaning back, sliding down in the seat. It actually gets to the point where I am moving around so much that people ask me to stop. The guy who usually sits next to me during the safety briefs will literally put his hand down to steady things and tell me I am shaking the entire table and ask if I can please just hold still. And it is so embarrassing because I genuinely did not even know I was doing it! \n\nBut I just have all this built-up energy, and if I am not physically walking around, it has to leak out somehow. So then I will try to sit on my hands to stop picking at my shirt or clicking the pen, but then my foot just immediately starts bouncing all over again, and it is just this never-ending cycle of trying not to bother anyone while feeling like my motor is just running and running. It happens at restaurants with friends, too, like I'm playing with the straw wrappers or shaking the booth, but it's the worst at work during those meetings because everyone is so quiet and just staring at the supervisor, and I'm over there practically vibrating.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding or really struggling to stick with tasks that require a lot of focus or mental effort, like reading something boring, filling out paperwork, or trying to follow complicated instructions?\n\nPatient: Oh my gosh, yes. That is honestly one of the biggest roadblocks for me right now, and it is so incredibly frustrating. Because, like I was saying before about wanting to get those certifications for the CNC machines and the forklifts? You can't just hop on the equipment and start pulling levers. There is this whole massive binder of training pages you have to get through first, plus all the regular paperwork I already have to do for my current line work, like the inventory logs. \n\nAnd I really, really try. I promise you, I do. I sit down, I tell myself, \"Okay, you're going to read this, you're going to focus, you're going to get this done so you can move up and make better money.\" I start out trying so hard. I'll have my pen ready, I'll be looking right at the page... but then, I don't know, maybe just a few minutes go by, and it's like my brain just completely unplugs itself. The numbers on the inventory sheets or the words in the CNC training manual, they literally just start to blur together. \n\nIt's not my eyes or anything, but it's like I'm suddenly looking at a completely foreign language. I'll be reading, or at least my eyes are moving across the page, and I'll get to the bottom, and I will suddenly realize I have absolutely no idea what I just looked at. Nothing. I didn't take a single word of it in. \n\nSo then I have to go back to the top and start all over again. And I'll do that three, four, five times for the exact same paragraph! It takes me an hour to do something that should take ten minutes because I just cannot hold onto the information. And with the inventory logs at the plant, it's the exact same thing. I'll be counting boxes of parts, and I'll have the clipboard, and I'll look down to write the number, and poof, the numbers just scramble in my head, and I have to recount the whole entire pallet. My supervisor gets so annoyed because I'm always the last one to turn my paperwork in at the end of the shift, but I just... I can't force my brain to stick with it, no matter how much I want to. I'll even try reading out loud to myself to see if that helps, but then I just start thinking about how my voice sounds in the empty warehouse, or if anyone can hear me, and then I've completely lost my place again. It's just exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times, outside of work or school, where you've had trouble staying still, focusing, or felt mentally restless, like in a doctor's waiting room, on public transport, or during a religious service?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely. I mean, waiting rooms are an absolute nightmare for me, and public transit? Forget about it. If I have to take the bus to the warehouse because my car is acting up or whatever, I am just miserable the entire time. I'll be sitting there in those uncomfortable plastic seats, and the bus is stopping at every single block, and I just feel this crazy urge to stand up and walk down the aisle, even though the bus is moving and you're supposed to stay seated. \n\nBut honestly, even though you asked about outside of work, my mind just keeps going right back to the plant because that is where this feeling is just... it's the most intense, and it's what I'm stressing about the most. Because it's not just a mental thing where I'm bored. When I try to stand still by the machine on the line, or when I'm trying to sit through that certification training I was telling you about... it literally feels like there’s this actual pressure building in my body. \n\nIt's hard to explain, but it's like a soda bottle that someone just shook up as hard as they could, and the cap is still screwed on tight. The carbonation is just expanding, right? That is exactly what my chest and my arms and my legs feel like. It just builds and builds and builds, and it gets so incredibly unbearable that I absolutely have to move, or pace, or stretch, or just go do something right then. I can't just wait five minutes. I can't just tough it out until the end of the shift. \n\nIf I'm standing by the machine waiting for a cycle to finish, which only takes a couple of minutes, that pressure gets so bad I'll start doing these huge, exaggerated stretches, or I'll just start pacing up and down the concrete aisle. Or I'll go find a broom to sweep a corner by the loading dock that's already completely clean, just because I have to do *something* physical right at that exact second to let the pressure out. It’s like my brain is screaming at me that standing still is physically impossible. So yeah, it happens on the bus and at the doctor's office and stuff, but standing at my station or sitting in that training room when that pressure hits... it's just torture.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you've had trouble staying still or focusing in situations like religious services, waiting rooms, or public transportation?\n\nPatient: I mean... I feel like I just kind of talked about the bus and the waiting rooms a second ago, right? Like, I don't really have any other examples besides what I just said. \n\nI don't go to church or anything like that, so I don't have to worry about sitting still through religious services. My mom used to try and make me go when I was younger, back before I dropped out, but I haven't been in years. Sundays are basically my only day to just crash and do laundry before I have to be back on the production line on Monday morning. And honestly, I try to avoid waiting rooms as much as humanly possible anyway. Besides coming here to talk to you, I don't really go to the doctor because I can't afford to miss a shift at the warehouse just to sit around for an hour reading old magazines while my legs are doing that crazy bouncing thing. So I just don't put myself in those situations if I can help it. \n\nMy whole routine is basically just the plant, the bus, and my apartment. Sometimes I go to the grocery store, but that doesn't bother me because I'm pushing a cart and walking up and down the aisles, so I'm constantly moving around and grabbing things. I'm not just trapped in a chair, you know? So, yeah, I don't really know what else to tell you. I don't really have any other places where this happens because I don't really go anywhere else.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you’ve felt restless or had trouble staying still, even during leisure activities like playing video games, watching something on your phone, or relaxing at home?\n\nPatient: Relaxing? I mean, I don't really even know what that actually looks like for me, to be completely honest. Because any kind of quiet stuff, whether I'm at home or anywhere else, it just makes me feel totally trapped. \n\nLike, okay, take my break time at the plant, for example. You get thirty minutes, and you're supposed to just chill out, eat your sandwich, just have some downtime, right? And since I really, really need to get through that certification manual I was telling you about earlier, I'll take the big binder into the break room with me. I'll sit down at one of those sticky tables in the corner, and it's usually pretty quiet in there except for the hum of the vending machines, and I tell myself I'm just going to sit quietly and read. \n\nBut I just... I can't do the quiet. I absolutely can't. The second I try to just sit there and be still and quiet, I feel like I'm suffocating. So instead of just reading the manual like a normal person, I end up humming some random song to myself, or I start tapping my steel-toe boots against the metal legs of the table, which is super loud on that linoleum floor. Or if literally anybody walks in to grab a coffee or heat up their lunch, I will immediately just start talking to them. I'll talk their ear off about absolutely nothing just to break the silence. And if nobody comes in, and it's just me and the quiet? I end up just getting up and walking out. I'll go pace the hallway instead of just being quiet. \n\nAnd it's the exact same way at home with my phone or video games or whatever. I can't just sit on the couch in the quiet and watch a video. If I try, I'm tapping my fingers on the back of the phone, or I'm shaking my leg so hard the whole couch vibrates, or I just end up getting up and wandering around my tiny apartment while the video is playing in my hand. It's like the quiet itself is just... it's like a cage, you know? I just can't do it. I have to make noise or move or talk to somebody.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've noticed these same difficulties—like trouble focusing, feeling restless, or acting without thinking—in at least two other distinct situations or environments outside of work and home, such as when you're out running errands, interacting with strangers, or during structured activities like classes or group events?\n\nPatient: I feel like... I mean, I know I just kind of said this a minute ago, but I really, truly don't have any other places to tell you about. You're asking for two other distinct situations, but my life just doesn't have two other situations in it. \n\nLike, you mentioned classes? I haven't been in a classroom since I dropped out. I couldn't handle sitting at a desk all day back then, and there is absolutely no way I'm signing up for any kind of structured class now. Plus, working the shifts I do at the plant, when would I even go to a class? I'm either on the production line, or I'm at home trying to recover from being on the production line. \n\nAnd group events... I don't really do those either. Honestly, by the time the weekend rolls around, my whole body is just completely physically exhausted from standing on that concrete floor all week, but my brain is still doing a million miles an hour. So I just end up pacing around my tiny apartment or messing with my phone. But you said outside of home and work, so that doesn't count for what you're asking. \n\nFor errands, I literally only go to the grocery store. And like I told you before, I'm pushing a cart the whole time, so I'm constantly walking and grabbing boxes and throwing them in the cart, so I don't get that awful trapped feeling there. I don't go to the bank, I just use the app on my phone. I don't go to the post office. I don't go to the movies because sitting in the dark for two hours sounds like absolute torture, so I haven't done that in years. I just automatically avoid anywhere that I know is gonna make me feel like I have to stay put or follow rules about being quiet. \n\nSo, yeah, I really don't interact with strangers or go to structured things. It's literally just work, the bus, and my apartment. I don't know what else to tell you because I just don't put myself in any other environments to even know if I'd have trouble focusing or feeling restless there. There's just... there's really nowhere else.\n\nDoctor: Can you remember having these same kinds of struggles with staying still, focusing, or acting without thinking before age 12?\n\nPatient: Oh, god, yeah. Definitely. I mean, honestly, it was probably even worse back then because I didn't have any control over where I was or what I was doing, you know? Like, in elementary school, I vividly remember third and fourth grade just being an absolute nightmare for me and my teachers. \n\nI was 100% that kid who just could not, for the life of me, stay in my seat. They used to try all these different things, like my third-grade teacher actually put masking tape on the floor around my desk to give me a \"boundary\" to stay inside of, but it never worked. I’d always end up wandering around the room anyway. I'd go to the pencil sharpener like five times in an hour, or I'd just get up and walk over to look out the window or check on the class hamster or whatever, just because I physically couldn't sit in that hard plastic chair for one more second without feeling like I was going to explode. \n\nAnd the talking... oh man, the talking. I talked over the teacher constantly. Like, they would be right in the middle of a sentence explaining a math problem or reading a story, and if I knew the answer, or honestly even if I just had some completely random thought pop into my head about what we were having for lunch, I would just blurt it out. I didn't even raise my hand. I didn't even realize I was doing it half the time until the teacher would stop talking and just glare at me, and the whole class would turn around and look. \n\nMy mom used to get so mad because I got notes sent home constantly. Like, it felt like almost every single week there was a new note stapled to my homework folder about how I was being \"disruptive\" or blurting things out during reading time or refusing to stay seated during lessons. It was just this endless cycle of getting in trouble, getting a note sent home, getting yelled at by my mom, and then going back the next day and doing the exact same thing because I just couldn't stop myself. So, yeah, this isn't a new thing at all. It’s basically been happening my entire life, way before I was twelve.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a time when you had trouble focusing or staying on task, even when you weren’t moving around or feeling restless?\n\nPatient: Oh, yeah, absolutely. It's not always just my body needing to move, sometimes it's like my brain just completely unplugs itself even if I'm standing perfectly still. \n\nLike, at the plant, sometimes my supervisor will have me do these inventory logs. It usually happens when a machine goes down and we're just waiting on maintenance, so they hand me this beat-up clipboard and tell me to go count boxes of fasteners or brackets or whatever in the storage cage. And I'm just standing there, not pacing, not moving around, just staring at the clipboard. I start out really trying, you know? I tell myself, \"Okay, just focus, count the boxes, write the number down, get it done.\" \n\nBut then... I don't know, it's like after just a few minutes, the numbers just completely lose all meaning. The lines on the paper start to blur together. I'll count a stack of boxes, look down at the sheet, and realize I completely forgot the number I literally just counted three seconds ago. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with that certification manual I was telling you about earlier. The CNC training pages. I really, really want to pass this test because getting certified on the CNC machines means a pretty decent pay bump, and my landlord just raised my rent again, so I really need the money. So I will sit there, forcing myself to be still, staring right at the training pages. I'm looking at the words, my eyes are moving across the sentences, I'm reading about feed rates and spindle speeds and tolerances... but my brain isn't actually absorbing a single thing. \n\nAfter a couple of minutes, the words just turn into gray mush on the page. They just blur together. I'll get all the way to the bottom of a really dense page about, I don't know, tool offsets, and I'll suddenly realize I have absolutely no idea what I just read. None. I didn't take any of it in. It's like my eyes were reading but my brain was on another planet. So then I have to go all the way back to the top of the page and start over. And then I'll do it again! I'll read the exact same paragraph four or five times in a row, just trying to force the information into my head, but it just won't stick. It is so incredibly frustrating because I am trying so hard, I'm putting in the effort, I'm not even moving, but it's like there's a wall in my head that just blocks the information from actually getting in."
